- **Ticker pause button:** a 44px button at the ticker's right edge stops and restarts the moving pairs, which closes the WCAG gap. It only appears where the ticker moves.
- **Vendor colours:** each vendor keeps one colour on every screen, set by upload order. Ranking now decides only the listing order (best first in the pair panel and the vendor switch), so the demo's Atlas Resolve is plum everywhere.
- **Pairs opens on disagreements:** from the Overview, the dock's Pairs link opens the vendor's disagreements when it has any, as in the design. It falls back to all pairs when there are none, so it never opens on an empty list.
- **Clearer wording:**
  - when two values differ only in capitals, the note now says "same words, different capitals"; other format differences still say "written differently";
  - the column toggle now reads "Show the 1 column that wasn't sent".
- **Privacy and Terms pages:** new `/privacy` and `/terms`, written in plain words from what the app actually does. I checked the claims: for example, deleting an evaluation does delete its records, vendor files and results.
  - They're linked from a new Legal column in the footer, the phone footer, and the line under the sign-up form ("By creating an account you agree to the Terms and Privacy policy").
- **Headline font:** Bricolage is now held at the open optical size the design was drawn with, which fixes the narrower-text difference on every screen. The hero's first line now measures 410px against the design's 416px.
- **Phone top bar:** down from three rows to two (about 115px). On phones only the current page's name and the demo badge stay, since the logo already leads home.
- **Accessibility test:** the demo accessibility checks now run at the end of each page, where the floating dock rests below the content. Mid-scroll, the dock covering a link had made the check flaky in Chromium and Firefox.

**Still open**
1. **Legal review:** both pages need it before launch. I didn't invent liability or legal clauses; there's a TODO in each page.
2. **Company legal name:** I still need it for the footer's © line and the legal pages.
3. **Security and Contact:** still not linked, because those pages don't exist.

The landing page now scrolls smoothly, the walkthrough and bars ease between positions instead of jumping, every clickable or hoverable item responds, and the closing mosaic's tiles pop on hover. Nothing is committed and your :3000 server is still up.

**Checks:**
- Lint passes and all 373 unit tests pass.
- The landing and public specs pass in Chromium, WebKit and Firefox (27 of 27), and the demo spec passes too (12 of 12).
- Under reduced motion none of this runs, as before.

**Scrolling:**
- **The whole landing page** now scrolls smoothly with the mouse wheel. Touch, keyboard and screen-reader scrolling are left native.
- **The walkthrough, the "Every figure…" bars, the drifting rings and the card fade-ins** used to jump straight to wherever the scroll put them. A wheel moves in 100px steps, which is what looked like "keyframe after keyframe". Each now glides to its new position over a fraction of a second.
- **The easing runs on time, not frames,** so it feels the same at any frame rate. That mattered: headless Firefox runs at only 4 frames a second, and a first version based on frames crawled there.
- **Section links glide** to their section, update the address, and move keyboard focus there.

**A bug I found and fixed:** after an instant jump, such as dragging the scrollbar or pressing Home, the smooth-scroll library kept a stale idea of where the page was. Clicking "Inside" from the FAQ then sent you to the bottom of the page. The landing now handles its own section links and always starts from the page's real position.

**Hover and press:**
- **Every link and button on the landing** now eases on hover and shrinks slightly while pressed: the top bar's links and logo, Sign in, the vendor switch, the ticker's pause button, "Skip the walkthrough", the figures, the FAQ questions and the footer links.
- **Specific touches:**
  - the vendor switch's border darkens on hover;
  - the theme switch's half-moon turns 40°, as in the design;
  - the question tiles shade in.
- **Hover-only items react too:**
  - ticker pairs lift 2px;
  - keycaps press down;

- **The "Two views. One decision." mosaic's tiles** pop to 150% under the pointer, like the hero's.

I've fixed the cause of that error, but I couldn't make it happen on my side, so please try Download again and let me know if it comes back. Nothing is committed.

**Cause:** our dropdown menus opened as "modal", and a modal Radix menu marks everything else on the page as hidden from screen readers. The Download button sits in the dock, which works before the rest of the Results page has finished loading. If you opened the menu in that gap, the menu changed the page header before the page had loaded it. React then saw a header that didn't match what the server sent and showed that warning. A slow dev server makes the gap wider, which is likely why you hit it and my test didn't.

**Fix** (`src/components/ui/dropdown-menu.tsx`): menus are now non-modal by default, so opening one never touches the rest of the page. This also covers the run switcher, the account menu and the menu in the evaluations table. A menu doesn't need to be modal:
- focus still moves into it, and the arrow keys and Esc work;
- focus goes back to the button when it closes;
- a click outside still closes it.

The one difference is that a click outside now also reaches whatever you clicked.

**Checks:**
- With the menu open, nothing on the page is marked hidden.
- The CSV, Excel and PDF downloads all save without errors.
- Lint and TypeScript are clean, and the production build succeeds.
- In Chromium, all 37 full-flow tests and all 30 demo-site and public-page tests pass.

I cleaned up the test data and stopped the test servers afterwards; the "failed" notices are from that.

# PairSight UX audit

**What I checked:** every demo screen, both sign-in pages, the 404 page and the landing page, on the running app at :3000. Desktop was 1440px in light mode and phone was 390px in dark mode. I also checked the keyboard tab order and the console. Items you've already set aside (human review, going live, analytics) are left out of the proposals.

## Overall: 67/85, B (good)

This is a credible, honest and well-crafted product. Its weak spot is the phone, where the two most important things on Results, the comparison numbers and the pair names, can't be read. The second issue is that the Overview's vendor selection is easy to miss.

| Framework | Score |
|---|---|
| 7 UX factors | 28/35 |
| 5 usability characteristics | 20/25 |
| 5 interaction dimensions | 19/25 |

### The 7 factors

| Factor | Score | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Useful | 4 | It answers the real question ("which vendor, and where to look") with a ranked verdict, a "where to look first" list and pairs you can open. |
| Usable | 4 | There's a clear 5-step dock and a single next action on each step; one weak spot is the vendor switch on the Overview (P1-2). |
| Findable | 3 | The vendor selection is hidden. Step 3 also has two names: "What Jev checks" in the dock, "What should Jev compare?" as the page title. |
| Credible | 5 | Lines like "show where to look, not who's right" and "Jev is an AI and can be wrong". It says exactly what gets sent and that record IDs never are. |
| Desirable | 4 | The redesign is polished: mosaic, glyphs, motion. |
| Accessible | 4 | Axe passes in both themes on desktop and phone, every screen works by keyboard, and there's a skip link. Against that, the phone hides the numbers (P1-1). |
| Valuable | 4 | Cost and time are shown before you start, and a run carries on in the background. |

### Usability and interaction design

- **Effectiveness 4, error tolerance 4:** the upload checks ("Every row has a record ID…"), settings that are fixed once a run starts, and retrying a failed pair all prevent or recover from mistakes.
- **Efficiency 4:** ↑/↓/Enter, J/K, `/` for search and `?` for all shortcuts.
- **Engagement 4, learning 4.**
- **Words 4, visual 4, time 4, behaviour 4.**
- **Physical space 3:** the phone problems below.

## Issues, by priority

**P1: fix next**

1. **On the phone, the Overview comparison table shows no numbers.** The table is 640px wide inside a 356px box and scrolls sideways with nothing to say so. You see "Overall score", "Correct merges" and the other labels, but every vendor's figures are off-screen.
   - *Fix:* under 600px, stack each measure: the label, then one line per vendor with its figure and the "higher" chip.
2. **The Overview's vendor switch is easy to miss.** The lower half ("Where to look first", "If the cut-off moved", "Why they disagree") follows the selected vendor card. Today it opened on Meridian Match while the headline praises Atlas Resolve. The only sign of selection is a slightly darker border, so nobody will guess the cards are a switch.
   - *Fix:* add a visible "Showing: [Atlas | Meridian]" switch above the lower half, or a clear selected state on the card.
3. **On the phone, Review rows are unreadable.** Both name columns are cut to about 9 characters ("Dexcor Sec…" next to "DEXCOR SEC…"), so the rows all look alike and the difference underlines disappear.
   - *Fix:* on the phone, use one cell per pair, with record A above record B at full width and the score on the right.

**P2: soon**

4. **The Home cards wrap and fall out of line.** Vendor names break across two lines ("Meridian / Match", "Clearline / Resolve") and the run date breaks after "run". The third card's taller rows push its summary line out of line with the other two cards. That goes against your no-wrapping rule for tight spots. The summary "86 and 97 disagreements" also doesn't say which number belongs to which vendor.
   - *Fix:* truncate names with the full name on hover, let the bar give way to the name, and write "Atlas 86 · Meridian 97".
5. **Step 3 has two names.** Pick one, and use it in the dock, the dock's Next button and the page title.

**P3: polish**

6. **The pair page lists fields in file order,** so differences and missing values sit between matching ones. With 11 fields that's fine; with 30 the important rows get buried.
   - *Fix:* sort differences first, or make the "Same 5 · Different 0 · Missing…" counts act as filters.
7. **Check and start: when nothing is new, the cost reads oddly.** "New checks 0 · About $0.00" looks like the run is free. In that case, say "Nothing new for Jev to check".
8. **The dock comes last in the tab order.** On the Overview it takes 40 or more Tab presses to reach Download.
   - *Fix:* a shortcut, or a second skip link to the page actions.

The overlap between the Next.js badge and the dock on the phone appears only in development, so it isn't a finding.

## Proposed order

1. **Phone Results (P1-1 and P1-3):** two layout changes under 600px, about half a day.
2. **Vendor switch, Home cards and step name (P1-2, P2-4, P2-5):** about a day.
3. **Polish (P3-6 to P3-8)** when convenient.

**Research:** before Phase 2, a 5-person task test would confirm P1-2. Give people three tasks: "which vendor is better", "show me the other vendor's misses", and "find a pair both got wrong".

**Limits:** this was an expert review on demo data with a dev server, not testing with real users. The signed-in flows (upload, column mapping, run progress) weren't re-walked here. They're covered by the 37 full-flow tests, which pass.

Should I start on Phase 1? I can also turn this audit into a shareable page if you want to pass it on.

# PairSight UX re-audit

**What I checked:**
- **Already-fixed screens:** everything from the first audit, re-checked after all three phases.
- **Not covered first time:** the signed-in journey on your local app, from sign-up through the welcome step, the empty account, new evaluation, a bad upload, a good upload, column mapping and a bad vendor file. Plus the landing page.
- **Sizes:** desktop 1440px and phone 390px.

## Overall: 69/85, B (up from 67)

| Framework | Before | Now |
|---|---|---|
| 7 UX factors | 28 | 29 (Findable 3→4) |
| 5 usability characteristics | 20 | 20 |
| 5 interaction dimensions | 19 | 20 (Physical space 3→4) |

All 8 earlier findings are fixed, plus the default vendor and the tablet top bar.

The signed-in journey holds up well:
- **Sign-up:** errors are specific and inline ("Use at least 8 characters", "Enter a valid email, like name@company.com"), and so is the welcome step.
- **New evaluation:** the dialog checks the name in place.
- **Locked steps:** the dock says what's missing ("Upload your records to carry on").
- **Too few rows:** the upload error explains why at least 200 records are needed.
- **Bad vendor file:** you're told what's wrong, with ways to fix it.
- **Record type:** detection says "These look like organisations to us… change it if we got it wrong."

## New findings

**P1**
1. **The empty Home page shows the old design.** The "What results look like" preview is the pre-redesign mockup, with the sidebar, the old filter pills and "Customer master — Q3 vendor check". It's the first thing every new account sees, and it doesn't look like what they'll get.
   - *Fix:* rebuild it from the new parts (a vendor card with its mosaic, plus a few measures), using the demo's real figures.

**P2**

2. **On a signed-in phone, the evaluation name is cut to "Su…".** The bar fits the wordmark, breadcrumb, Runs, theme switch and account on one row.
   - *Fix:* inside an evaluation, show only the logo tiles on phones, or give the breadcrumb its own row as on tablets.
3. **Upload errors appear partly under the dock.** "A few things to fix first" shows up at the bottom of the screen with its last line under the dock's hint bubble, and nothing moves you to it.
   - *Fix:* scroll it into view above the dock when it appears, and make sure screen readers announce it.
4. **The upload says "Uploading your file… 100%" for several seconds after the upload is done,** while the file is being read. Only the dock's hint says "still reading".
   - *Fix:* once the upload reaches 100%, the card itself should say "Reading your file…".

**P3**

5. **The mapped-field chips clip silently on a phone** ("Record ID · Organisation name · Trading name · Regis"). The single line is your rule, and "Review columns" shows them all.
   - *Fix:* add a fade at the edge, or a "+4 more" chip.
6. **One hydration warning, not reproduced.** It appeared once on the first walkthrough, during a dev compile. Visiting every page again didn't bring it back, and the time-of-day greeting is already safe. I'd keep an eye on it rather than act now.

**Limits:**
- I didn't finish the vendor-file success path myself: my script picked the wrong file input after the bad file. The 37 full-flow tests cover that path, and they pass.
- I stopped before Start run, because your dev server uses the real Jev.
- **Leftover test data:** the walkthroughs created three throwaway accounts (`e2e+ux-…@pairsight.test`) on your local database. They have small draft evaluations and never ran anything, so there's no AI usage. I can delete them if you'd like; I haven't touched them.

Should I fix 1–5 as one batch?

# PairSight UX audit, third pass

**Score: 70/85, B (up from 69).** I walked the signed-in journey end to end against the Jev stub (no credits): vendor file, editing the settings, Check and start, starting a run, the Runs panel, Results, the pair page on a phone, deleting an evaluation, and a missing-evaluation 404. The flow ran in 45 seconds, and the only console error was the deliberate 404. Error handling moves up a point.

It holds up well:
- **Settings:** emptying the answer gives an inline "Tell Jev when two records are the same", and every saved change offers Undo.
- **Runs panel:** says what a run is and that it carries on in the background.
- **Delete:** the confirmation names the evaluation and says its uploads, runs and results go with it.
- **Missing evaluation:** the 404 is clear and offers a way back.

## Findings

All four were small and clear-cut, so I fixed them in this pass rather than waiting for your go-ahead. Nothing is committed.

1. **The Undo toast outlived its page (P2).** "Change saved — Undo" from the settings page stayed up through Check and start, the Runs panel and Results. Pressing Undo there would quietly change settings after a run had already started with them. The settings form now closes its Undo toast when you leave the page. It still saves your last change on the way out, as before.
2. **"0 of these 1 pairs" (P3).** A vendor file with one pair now reads "This pair wasn’t already suggested by PairSight", or "was". The note underneath no longer always says "The rest are added". It now follows how many are left:
   - **None:** "Every vendor match is already among the pairs to check"
   - **One:** "The one that wasn’t is added…"
   - **Several:** "The rest are added…"
3. **Dialogs opened on the close button (P3).** The shared Dialog now opens on the first field, otherwise on the first action, which is the safe one:
   - **New evaluation:** the name field.
   - **Delete, Replace file and Remove vendor:** Cancel.
   - **Runs panel:** the Active/Completed switch.
4. **Noted, not changed:** "Where are these records from?" defaulted to United States for a UK file. That's most likely my test browser's US language setting, not a bug. PairSight could suggest the country from the file's postcodes and phone numbers, which the new profiling already reads. That's a feature rather than a fix, so I've left it.

**Checks:** the three fixes are confirmed in the browser, and New evaluation opens on its name field. Lint, formatting, 371 unit tests and the production build pass. In Chromium, all 31 demo-site and public tests and all 37 full-flow tests pass. The test data is cleaned up.

**Files changed:**
- `ui/dialog.tsx`
- `rules/rules-form.tsx`
- `vendors/vendor-file.tsx`
- `messages/en.json`

These walkthroughs added a few more throwaway `e2e+ux…@pairsight.test` accounts, some on your :3000 database. Say if you want them removed.

# PairSight complete UX evaluation: report only, nothing changed

**Overall: 70/85, B (good).** The product is honest and well-crafted, and works on every screen size. What's left is mostly first-run guidance and small wording and polish items.

**What this draws on:**
- Four audit passes this session: the demo, the signed-in journey through to a finished run (against the stub), the landing page, the legal pages, the cut-off drawer, the shortcuts panel, and keyboard use.
- Sizes: desktop 1440px, tablet 768px, phone 390px. Both themes.

## Who it's for (working personas)
- **Priya, data steward:** compares two vendors' matching on a customer master and new to matching terms. She needs to know which vendor to trust and which pairs to look at, and she'll quit on jargon or setup she doesn't understand.
- **Tom, data engineer:** reruns checks at different cut-offs, uses the keyboard, and downloads findings. He needs speed, exact numbers and an export.

## Scores

| 7 UX factors | Score | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Useful | 4 | Answers "which vendor, and where do I look" with a ranked verdict and pairs you can open |
| Usable | 4 | 5-step dock, one next action per step, locked steps say why |
| Findable | 4 | Vendor switch, one name per step, skip link to actions |
| Credible | 5 | "Shows where to look, not who's right"; says exactly what Jev sees; plain Privacy page |
| Desirable | 4 | Polished redesign, mosaic, motion |
| Accessible | 4 | Axe passes in both themes on desktop and phone; full keyboard use; reduced motion respected |
| Valuable | 4 | Cost and time shown before starting; runs carry on in the background |
| **Total** | **29/35** | |

| Usability characteristic | Score | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Effectiveness | 4 | Full run walked end to end with no errors (the country default can quietly skew results, issue 1) |
| Efficiency | 4 | ↑/↓/Enter, J/K, `/`, `?`; autosave |
| Engagement | 4 | |
| Error tolerance | 5 | Inline validation, Undo on settings, delete confirmation naming what goes, retry for failed pairs |
| Ease of learning | 4 | Plain copy and a demo; no sample file to start from (issue 2) |
| **Total** | **21/25** | |

| Interaction dimension | Score | Weak spot |
|---|---|---|
| Words | 4 | "About $0.0031", "Country: US" |
| Visual | 4 | Cut-off drawer half-covers text |
| Physical space | 4 | Tablet and phone fixed; small leftovers |
| Time | 4 | "Reading your file…" fixed; a long run's progress is unchecked here |
| Behaviour | 4 | |
| **Total** | **20/25** | |

## Issues and proposed solutions

**P1: worth doing next**

1. **The country defaults to United States, whatever the file.** The column default is "US", and profiling never suggests a country. A UK file (UK postcodes, numbers like `0141 496 1439`) set up as "United States" gets its local phone numbers read the US way when picking pairs, and Jev is told the records are from the US. Many users won't open that dropdown.
   - *Proposal:* during profiling, suggest a country from the file: postcode shapes, phone prefixes (+44, leading 0 with UK area codes), country columns, TLDs like `.co.uk`. Pre-fill it and say so ("These look like UK records. Change it if not."), the way record-type detection already works. If nothing is clear, leave it unset and require a choice on the settings page.
   - *Effort:* about 1 day. The phone library is already there.

**P2: soon**

2. **No sample file to start from.** The Records drop zone says "One row per record, with column names in the first row", but a first-time user can't see what a good file or vendor file looks like without the demo.
   - *Proposal:* add "Download a sample" links under each drop zone: a small records CSV and both vendor formats (pairs and groups), cut from the demo data. Half a day.
3. **The cut-off drawer half-covers the page.** Opened, it sits over the headline, and a sliver of text ("pairs only.") shows cut off beneath its edge. It looks like a rendering fault.
   - *Proposal:* give the drawer a solid full-width background with a soft shadow at its lower edge, or let it push the content down while open. Under half a day.
4. **"About $0.0031" reads oddly** on Check and start. Four decimals of a dollar is a precision nobody needs.
   - *Proposal:* round to a cent and floor it: "Less than 1¢" under a cent, otherwise "About $0.42". About 1 hour.

**P3: polish**

5. **The pair-page legend leads with zeros** ("Different 0"). *Proposal:* show zero counts in muted text, but keep them; "no differences" is itself useful to know.
6. **The run details show raw codes:** "Country: US" and a full run ID. *Proposal:* show the country's name, and keep the run ID behind a copy button or in a tooltip.
7. **"All caught up" takes a large card on Home** when nothing needs picking up. *Proposal:* collapse it to one line. This conflicts with your "dashboard sizes stay fixed" preference, so it's your call.
8. **Deleting is permanent,** though well guarded by the confirmation. *Proposal:* soft delete with a 10-second "Undo" toast, removing for good after that. 1–2 days; low priority because the confirmation already names what goes.
9. **The one hydration warning,** seen once during a dev compile. Not reproduced in four passes; keep an eye out, nothing to do now.

**Known open items (yours to schedule):**
- the company legal name in the footer and legal pages;
- a legal review of Privacy and Terms;
- no Contact or Security page.

These matter only once PairSight is shown outside the team. Password reset and SSO stay out, as you decided.

## Redesign themes behind the fixes
- **Setup that guesses well and says so** (1, 2): the app already detects the record type and dates. Extending that to the country, and offering samples, makes the first ten minutes easier for Priya.
- **Results that read cleanly** (3–6): no half-hidden text, no false precision, no raw codes. Small items that add up to trust.

## Suggested order
1. Issue 1, country suggestion (about 1 day).
2. Issues 2–4 together (about 1 day).
3. P3 items as convenient.

**Research:** one 5-person usability test with Priya-type users would cover what this review can't. Tasks:
- set up an evaluation from their own file;
- say which vendor is better;
- find a pair both vendors got wrong.

Watch for whether they notice the country setting, and how long the first upload takes them.

**Limits:**
- This is an expert review on demo and test data, not real users.
- The stub finishes runs in about a second, so the progress screen for a long run wasn't seen in use.
- I didn't change anything this pass.

I worked through the list in order and finished everything except item 7, which needs your call. Nothing is committed.

**1. The country is set from the file.**
- **What changed:** when records are saved, PairSight now works out where they're from. It only counts clear evidence: a country column, international phone numbers (`+44…`), postcodes only one country uses (UK, Canada, Ireland, the Netherlands, US ZIP+4), and country email and website domains (`.co.uk`, `.ie`…). It needs at least 10 such signals, 60% of them for one country; otherwise it leaves the setting alone.
- **Your choice is never overwritten:** the first save sets the country. Replacing the file later only changes it if it's still PairSight's own earlier suggestion. No database change was needed; the suggestion lives in the file's saved profile.
- **What you see:** on Records, "Where they’re from: United Kingdom, going by their postcodes, so we read local phone numbers and dates the way they’re written there. If that’s wrong, change it in What Jev checks." On the settings page, under the dropdown: "We set this from the postcodes in your file; change it if that’s wrong."
- **Tests:** 5 new unit tests cover each signal, too little evidence, a tie, and the things that prove nothing (five-digit postcodes, local phone numbers, `.io`).
- **Side effect:** the e2e padding's filler postcodes look British, so the padded test files are now treated as UK. All tests still pass.

**2. Sample files.** Records now says "Not sure how it should look? Download a sample file of 2,000 made-up organisations, the same as the demo’s." Vendors offers samples as a list of pairs or a list of groups, which match the sample records, so all three run end to end. They're generated from the demo by `pnpm samples:build` into `public/samples/`.

**3. The cut-off drawer** now has a soft shade under its edge, stronger in dark mode, so the page reads as going underneath it instead of being cut off.

**4. The cost** shows "Less than 1¢" under a cent, otherwise "About $0.42", rounded to whole cents.

**5. The pair-page legend** keeps zero counts, but muted.

**6. Run details** show the country's name ("United Kingdom") and a short run ID with a Copy button. The copy puts the full ID on the clipboard and confirms with a toast.

**8. Deleting has Undo.** Once you confirm, the evaluation disappears from everywhere on Home: the cards, "Pick up where you left off" and the table. A "“Name” deleted — Undo" toast stays for 10 seconds, and the delete only happens after that. Undo brings the evaluation back. Leaving the page during those 10 seconds still deletes it. If the delete fails, the evaluation comes back with the reason. The delete test now checks Undo, then a second delete going through once the 10 seconds are up.

**Not done:**
- **7.** Shrinking the large "All caught up" card goes against your rule that Home's card sizes stay fixed. Should I collapse it to one line?
- **9.** The one-off hydration warning: just keep an eye on it.

**Checks:** lint, TypeScript, formatting, line endings, 376 unit tests and the production build pass. The build first failed because the React Compiler can't handle one loop shape in the new delete code; I rewrote that loop. In Chromium, all 37 full-flow tests and all 31 demo-site and public tests pass. I also walked the new pieces in the browser using the sample files themselves. Test data is cleaned up.

**New files:** `country-guess.ts` and its test, `pending-deletes.tsx`, `sample-link.tsx`, `runs/copy-id.tsx`, `scripts/build-samples.mts`, and the three files in `public/samples/`.

|---|---|---|
| Duplicates found, messy people file (10K) | 65.5% | **88.0%** |
| Duplicates found, messy people file (100K) | 52.6%, 137 s to score | **87.4%, 23 s** |
| Pairs scored to find them, messy people 10K | 69,715 | **16,991** |
| Script protection | standard CSP | **fresh nonce on every page view**; e2e fails on any violation |
| CodeQL findings fixed | not run | **9 real ones** (8 unpinned CI actions, 2 shell-interpolation spots) |
| UX audit score | not measured | **67 → 70 / 85** (grade B) over four passes, every finding fixed or decided |
| Tests | 331 unit, 57 e2e | **376 unit, 68 e2e in Chromium** (+ walkthrough tests in WebKit and Firefox) |
| Phone (390px) | desktop layouts squeezed | **every screen designed for it**; comparison numbers and pair names readable |
| Country of records | always "United States" unless changed | **suggested from the file** (postcodes, phones, domains) |

## 8 Oct: PR #19 opened and fixed (merged 9 Oct)
- **What:** opened the PR for the ClickHouse-style UI revamp and the landing page (7 commits). The Python CI job failed in a cache step it never needed; it no longer enables the pnpm cache.
- **Numbers:** 331 unit tests and 57 e2e tests passed; then all 7 CI jobs went green.

## 9 Oct: PR #20, security and test fixes (merged)
- **Nonce CSP:**
  - **What:** every page only runs scripts carrying a fresh per-request nonce, so an injected script does nothing. The proxy's policy had been replacing the anti-framing rules instead of adding to them; both now share one definition. Every e2e test now fails on a CSP violation.
  - **Business:** closes the most common web-attack class before any customer sees the app.
- **CodeQL, first scan:**
  - 245 JS/TS and 233 Python queries, 84 findings to triage.
  - **Real ones fixed:** CI actions pinned to exact commits (7× `pnpm/action-setup`, 1× `claude-code-action`), and two dev scripts no longer pass arguments through a shell. An injection attempt (`;touch PWNED`) is now rejected.
  - **What's left:** 68 findings, all checked and false positives.
- **Windows and flaky tests:**
  - **Windows:** `check:standards` now runs on Windows, 24/24.
  - **Flaky 100K test:** it now waits up to 30 s instead of 5 s.
  - **Numbers:** 340 unit tests, 64/64 e2e.
- **Research:** whether profiling records before matching actually helps. Published studies say blanket cleaning barely helps or even hurts: Randall 2013 found heavy cleaning took accuracy from 0.883 to 0.875. Turning placeholder values into blanks helped every time. That shaped the plan.

## 10 Oct: PR #21, profiling before picking pairs (merged)
- **What:**
  - **Profiling:** every uploaded file is profiled once: how full each field is, its commonest values, placeholders like "N/A" or a switchboard number, and which way round dates are written.
  - **Cleaned keys:** the profile cleans only the keys used to pick pairs. Jev and every screen still see exactly what the file says.
  - **New Records card:** "What we noticed in this file".
  - **File reading:** Windows-encoded CSVs and Excel's zero-padded numbers are now read correctly. More company legal forms are dropped from names.
  - **Benchmark:** the files now plant real-world mess, so the numbers below are measured on dirty data.
- **Why:** real files are messy. Two strangers who both say "N/A" looked like a match, and dates PairSight couldn't read counted as no evidence.
- **Numbers (duplicates found, before → after):**

  | File | Before | After |
  |---|---|---|
  | People 10K, messy | 65.5% (69,715 pairs scored) | **88.0%** (16,991) |
  | People 100K, messy | 52.6%, 137 s | **87.4%, 23 s** |
  | Products 10K, messy | 95.3% | **97.9%** |
  | Organisations 10K, messy | 79.9% from 99,383 pairs | 79.9% from **5,997** |
  | Clean files | | unchanged |

- **Measured, not built:** having the profile steer the matching model. With placeholders blanked, no column is skewed any more, so it showed no gain.
- **Impact:**
  - **UX:** dirty customer files work almost as well as clean ones.
  - **Business:** better accuracy and 4–6× less scoring work on real-world data.
- **Numbers:** 363 unit tests, 66 e2e tests, 31 Python tests.

## 10–11 Oct: the redesign (uncommitted, `feat/redesign-foundations`)
- **Plan:** 7 stages and 12 conflicts with the design, each with a proposal. Your decisions:
  - the landing opens dark, and the product follows the system setting;
  - a two-way light/dark toggle;
  - the dock keeps our labels ("Next: vendors");
  - the handoff folder stays untracked.
- **Foundations:**
  - **Look:** the new palette (paper and charcoal, vermilion for the vendor, ice for Jev), three typefaces, and the light/dark switch.
  - **Components:** 44px two-part buttons, chips, the segmented control, keycaps, the three-tile logo, and the 22-column mosaic chart.
  - **Navigation:** the sidebar is replaced by a slim top bar and a floating dock.
- **Every screen restyled:** Results Overview, the Pairs list, the Pair page, Records, Vendors (now side-by-side cards, each a full editor), What Jev checks, Check and start, Home (demo and your own), and sign-in/sign-up. Screens with no design (errors, 404, run in progress, loading, empty states) were built from the new parts.
- **Landing page rebuilt on the demo's real data:**
  - a live mosaic hero with a vendor switch;
  - a pairs ticker;
  - a pinned "How it works" walkthrough that plays as you scroll;
  - FAQ and footer, plus new Privacy and Terms pages.
- **Motion:**
  - **What:** load animations, a mosaic ripple, a cursor glow, scroll-linked bars and figures, smooth scrolling, and hover and press effects on everything.
  - **Behaviour:** the same in Chrome, Safari (WebKit) and Firefox, and off when reduced motion is set.
  - **Your feedback:** the walkthrough "looks like keyframes after keyframes". Fixed with time-based easing.
- **Phone and accessibility pass:**
  - **Phone:** the landing follows the phone design, and every product screen works at 390px.
  - **Touch and keyboard:** 44px touch targets, a focus ring on every tab stop, and skip links.
  - **Animation:** app animations brought down to 240ms (up to 760ms before).
  - **Safari:** a dialog bug fixed.
- **Fixes from your testing:**
  - **Results:** badges no longer push vendor content out of line; "Look at these first" rows got hover padding; the side panel fits on screen, with the duplicate field rows removed. Vendor cards now react to hover: tiles pop and the legend highlights its category.
  - **Download error:** opening a menu before the page finished loading caused a hydration error; menus are now non-modal.
  - **Branding:** a new favicon and home-screen icon.
- **Impact:**
  - **UX:** one consistent, distinctive design across the whole product, usable on a phone and by keyboard.
  - **Business:** the landing now explains the product with real figures and can be shown to prospects.
- **Numbers:** 373 unit tests; 37 full-flow tests in Chromium; 66 tests across WebKit and Firefox.

## 12 Oct: four UX audit passes and their fixes (uncommitted)
- **Pass 1 (67/85)**, fixed in three phases:
  - **Phone:** the comparison table showed no numbers (a 640px table in a 356px box), and pair names were cut to about 9 characters. Both now readable.
  - **Overview vendor switch:** "Everything below is about [Atlas | Meridian]". Results now opens on the best-scoring vendor, at your request.
  - **Home cards:** they no longer wrap, and step 3 has one name.
  - **Pair page:** differences now come first.
  - **Run card:** "Jev has already checked every pair…" replaces "About $0.00".
  - **Keyboard:** a "Skip to page actions" link reaches the dock in one key press. Before, it took 40+ presses of Tab.
  - **Tablet:** the breadcrumb is no longer squeezed.
- **Pass 2 (69/85)**, first walk of the signed-in journey:
  - **Empty Home:** showed the old design's mockup; now it shows a picture of the real demo results.
  - **Phone:** the evaluation name was cut to "Su…"; it now gets about 136px.
  - **Upload errors:** they now scroll into view instead of landing under the dock.
  - **Upload label:** says "Reading your file…" after 100%.
  - **Field chips on a phone:** they now scroll and fade.
- **Pass 3 (70/85)**, run end to end on the stub:
  - **Undo:** the settings "Undo" toast no longer follows you onto later pages.
  - **Wording:** "0 of these 1 pairs" fixed.
  - **Dialogs:** they open on Cancel or the first field.
- **Pass 4 (report only, then worked in order):**
  - **Country suggested from the file:** postcodes, international phones, country domains or a country column; it never overrides your own choice. A UK file had defaulted to "United States", which misread local phone numbers.
  - **Sample files:** downloadable from Records and Vendors, with 2,000 made-up organisations plus both vendor formats.
  - **Cost:** shows "Less than 1¢" instead of "About $0.0031".
  - **Delete:** now has a 10-second Undo.
  - **Smaller items:** the cut-off drawer's edge is clearer, zero counts are muted, and run details show the country's name and a copyable run ID.
- **Impact:**
  - **UX:** first-time users get guidance (samples, sensible defaults, errors in view), mistakes can be undone, and phones work.
  - **Business:** fewer support questions and fewer silently wrong results from a wrong country.
- **Numbers:** 376 unit tests (5 new for the country guess); 37 full-flow tests and 31 demo and public tests pass.

## Decisions you made
- Password reset and SSO: left out for now.
- Results opens on the best-scoring vendor.
- No shareable report page for the audits.

PR 1 of the on-prem plan is done on `feat/onprem-jev-key`: an admin can now set the Jev key in the app. It's uncommitted for your review; nothing is committed or pushed.

**What you'll see**
- **Settings** is new in the account menu. Everyone signed in can see whether runs use a saved key (shown as "key ending ••••ab12"), the server's own key, or none. Only an admin can change it.
- **Saving a key:** an admin pastes it, and Jev's address defaults to TypeSafe. "Check and save" asks Jev about one made-up pair first, and only saves the key if Jev accepts it. The field then clears and the key is never shown again. "Remove key" goes back to the server's own key.
- **No key:** Check and start shows "Runs need a Jev key first. Add one in Settings", and the server also refuses to start the run. A run whose pairs were all checked before still starts, because it sends nothing to Jev.
- **A run stopped by a key problem** now says an admin can fix it in Settings, with a link.

**How it works**
- **Storage:** a new `jev_connections` table holds the key encrypted (AES-256-GCM) under a new `PAIRSIGHT_SECRET_KEY` setting, plus the last four characters for display. The table has RLS like every other, and its migration is generated.
- **Where calls get the key:** every Jev call asks `src/server/ai/jev-credentials.ts` for the saved key first, then falls back to the existing `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`. Usage is still costed the same way. The key check is costed too, under the feature name `pairsight_jev_key_check`.
- **Design change from the plan:** instead of a generic secrets table, I used one Jev connection per worker pool. Your dev server and the e2e server share a database, and this stops a key saved by e2e from changing your dev server's key.
- **Docs:** a new `docs/PairSight-OnPrem.md`, linked from AGENTS.md and the roadmap.

**Bugs found while testing**
- **Runs sometimes ignored a newly saved key.** Next loads the background worker and the Settings action as separate copies of the code, and saving only cleared the action's copy of the remembered key. So a run could keep using the old key for up to 30 seconds. The memory is now shared by both, the same way the run limiter already does it, and the test proves the run's calls carry the saved key.
- **A test that intermittently failed on main:** the dashboard test's "Evaluation restored" check sometimes also matched the toast's screen-reader copy. It's now an exact match.

**Checks:** typecheck, lint, all 380 unit tests (new encryption tests included), the RLS check (now 18 tables), the secret scan and the build pass. On a production build against the Jev stub, the e2e suites passed:
- the new Settings test;
- both PairSight shards (19 + 19);
- the pushback tests (5);
- the public and demo tests (22).

I also checked the page's screenshots in light, dark and at phone width. Afterwards I cleaned up the e2e AI usage rows, removed the screenshot account and stopped the servers. The few key-check rows from e2e aren't tied to a run, so that cleanup leaves them. They are stub calls with no cost.

**To try it on your dev server:**
1. Add an encryption key to `.env.local` and restart the dev server. You can generate one with:
   ```
   node -e "console.log(require('crypto').randomBytes(32).toString('base64'))"
   ```
2. Give your own account an admin seat. There's no screen for that until PR 2 (which makes the first sign-up on an install the admin), so for now it's this SQL with your email:
   ```sql
   INSERT INTO admin_members ("userId", role, "permissionGrants")
   SELECT id, 'admin', '{}' FROM users WHERE email = 'you@example.com';
   ```

Say when you'd like it committed and opened as a PR. After that comes PR 2: the offline profile, the first admin, and the bundled rate card.

PR 2 is done on the same branch as PR 1 (`feat/onprem-jev-key`). You can now test both together, starting from an empty install. Nothing is committed yet.

**What PR 2 adds:**
- **An on-prem setting:** `PAIRSIGHT_DEPLOYMENT=onprem`. Left unset, the hosted app behaves exactly as before.
- **The first account becomes the admin.** Two people signing up at the same moment can't both become admin. Later accounts are ordinary users.
- **People in Settings** (on-prem only):
  - an admin sees everyone with an account and can make them an admin or remove them;
  - an admin can never remove themselves, and the last admin can't be removed;
  - non-admins don't see the list.
- **Only Jev leaves the machine.** Usage costs are still recorded, using a bundled price list (`pnpm ai:rate-card`) instead of fetching OpenRouter's each day. `check:ai-models` now fails if that list is missing a model. At startup the server logs every outside service it will contact; on-prem that's just Jev.
- **The Privacy page** on-prem opens with "On your organisation’s own machine". It also says this copy doesn't count usage or report errors to us.

**Checks:**
- Lint, types, formatting, the env, secrets and standards checks, the RLS check (18 tables) and all 383 unit tests pass.
- **New on-prem e2e spec,** run against an on-prem server on an empty database. Both tests pass:
  1. the first account becomes admin;
  2. a second person can't change anything;
  3. making them admin and removing them works;

**To test it yourself:** I've made an empty, migrated database (`pairsight_onprem_try`) next to your dev one, and the build in `.next` is current. Run this in PowerShell from the repo; it uses port 3200, so your :3000 dev server isn't affected:

```powershell
$env:DATABASE_URL="postgresql://postgres:postgres@127.0.0.1:54322/pairsight_onprem_try"
$env:PAIRSIGHT_DEPLOYMENT="onprem"
$env:PAIRSIGHT_SECRET_KEY=(node -e "console.log(require('crypto').randomBytes(32).toString('base64'))")
pnpm start -p 3200
```

Then open http://localhost:3200:
1. Sign up; that first account is the admin.
2. Open Settings from the account menu. You'll see "No Jev key yet", the key form and People.
3. Sign up a second account in another browser profile and make it an admin from People.

A few things to know while testing:
- **Accounts:** sign-in is your local Supabase, so use emails you haven't signed up with on :3000.
- **Saving a key:** a real TypeSafe key spends one tiny check call. For a free check, run `node scripts/jev-stub.mjs` and set Jev's address to `http://127.0.0.1:4010/v1` (any key except `e2e-refused-key` is accepted).
- **Restarting:** keep the same PowerShell window. A new `PAIRSIGHT_SECRET_KEY` makes the saved key unreadable, and Settings will tell you so.

Yes, it used the key from your `.env` file. That's the fallback I built in, not a bug.

Runs use the key an admin saved in Settings first. If none is saved, they fall back to `TYPESAFE_API_KEY` from the server's environment (`src/server/ai/jev-credentials.ts:31`). `next start` loads `.env` and `.env.local` from the repo, so the :3200 test server found your dev key even though you never set one in the app. Settings would have shown this as "Runs use the Jev key this server was set up with." Those runs went to whichever Jev address your `.env` points at. If that's the real TypeSafe one, they used real credits.

A real customer install won't do this. The Compose stack in PR 3 never sets `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`, so the only key is the one the admin saves. Your local test just doesn't match that.

**To test it the way a customer would**, blank the key before starting the server, in the same PowerShell window:

```powershell
$env:TYPESAFE_API_KEY=""
pnpm start -p 3200
```

Next doesn't overwrite a variable that's already set, even when it's empty, and the app treats an empty key as no key. Settings will then show "No Jev key yet" and the Plan screen will block Start until you save one. The stub (`node scripts/jev-stub.mjs`, address `http://127.0.0.1:4010/v1`) costs nothing for the check and the runs.

**My recommendation:** make the on-prem setting ignore the server key altogether, so runs there only ever use the key an admin saved. That makes "the customer sets their own key" a guarantee rather than something that depends on how the server was configured. Hosted would keep the fallback. It's a small change plus one test. Should I make it?

Your :3000 and :3200 servers had already stopped, so I only stopped the local Supabase containers. `supabase start` brings them back with their data.

**What PR 3 adds**
- **The image** (`deploy/onprem/Dockerfile`): the web server, the run worker inside it, and Splink's Python. It runs as a non-root user and carries the built app, not the source. It's 1.63 GB, down from 2.74 GB on the first working version.
- **Start-up** (`deploy/onprem/entrypoint.sh`): each start applies migrations and the one-off SQL, loads the sandbox only the first time, then serves. Restarting changes nothing.
- **The stack** (`deploy/onprem/compose.yaml`):
  - Postgres, the sign-in server, Caddy and the app, with only `127.0.0.1:3080` published.
  - Sign-in is reachable only from the app container, never from the browser.
  - Accounts live in their own schema under their own database role.
- **Secrets:** `node scripts/onprem-env.mjs` writes each install's random secrets to `deploy/onprem/.env`. It refuses to overwrite an existing one, because new secrets would lock that install out of its data.
- **Testing:** `compose.test.yaml` adds the free Jev stub beside the stack.
- **CI** (`.github/workflows/onprem.yml`): builds the image, starts the stack and runs the on-prem tests, but only when the files it's built from change. It hasn't run on GitHub yet; it will once there's a PR.
- **Smaller changes:** `next.config.ts` builds Next's standalone output on-prem only; `demo-seed` gains `--if-missing`; the stub can listen on another address; `docs/PairSight-OnPrem.md` has a new section on the stack.

**Fixes needed along the way:**
- **Database roles:** the sign-in server expects a role named `postgres`, so the database now has three roles, each with its own password.
- **Prisma engines:** Prisma needed `openssl` at build time to download engines matching the runtime.
- **Missing run worker:** Next's standalone output leaves out `instrumentation`, which is what starts the run worker. With it missing, runs stuck on "Still looking at your records…". A small build step, `deploy/onprem/copy-instrumentation.mjs`, copies it back in.

**Checks:**
- **On-prem tests, against the stack on a fresh database:** all 3 pass, including the full sample-file run. That run completed with 210 pairs, 178 suggested by the image's own Splink.
- **Hosted:** lint, types, formatting, the env, secrets and standards checks, the unit tests (385 pass, plus the 1 skipped as before) and the Splink checks all pass. The hosted build compiles, without the standalone output.
- **Not run:** `check:rls` and the full hosted e2e suite, because local Supabase is stopped. Neither migrations nor hosted behaviour changed, and I'll run both before the PR.

**To try it** (the `:dev` image is built and `deploy/onprem/.env` already exists):

PR 4 is built on `feat/onprem-compose` alongside PR 3, uncommitted. The installer script works end to end against Docker here, but the `.exe` itself hasn't been compiled or run, because Inno Setup isn't installed on this machine.

**`deploy/windows/pairsight.ps1` does the real work.** It runs in Windows PowerShell 5.1, which every Windows 10 and 11 machine has.
- **Before installing (`check`):**
  - Docker Desktop is installed and running; the script starts it if it's installed but not running.
  - The machine has 8 GB of memory and 10 GB free.
  - Port 3080 is free.

  Each failure has its own exit code and a plain message.
- **Installing (`install`):**
  - Copies the stack into `%ProgramData%\PairSight` and locks that folder to Administrators, SYSTEM and the person installing.
  - Makes the secrets once and keeps them on every later install.
  - Backs up an existing install, then starts the stack.
  - If the data is there but its settings file is missing, it stops and explains, rather than making new secrets that would lock the data out.
- **Other commands:**
  - `start`, `stop`, `status`, `logs`
  - `backup`: a dump in `Documents\PairSight backups`
  - `restore -File`: backs up first
  - `update`
  - `uninstall`: keeps the data unless `-DeleteData`; backups are never deleted

**`deploy/windows/pairsight.iss` is the Inno Setup installer.** It's a thin wrapper:
- It runs `check` before copying anything and shows a clear reason for each failure.
- It runs `install` in a visible window, then offers to open PairSight in your browser.
- The Start menu gets Open, Stop and Back up, plus an optional desktop icon.
- Uninstalling asks whether to delete the data, with "no" as the default.
- The icon is made from the app's own one.

**Release and CI:**
- **Releases:** pushing a tag `onprem-v1.0.0` publishes the image to GHCR and builds `PairSight-Setup-1.0.0.exe`, pinned to that image's digest, on a GitHub Release. It's signed, uninstaller included, once the `WINDOWS_SIGNING_CERT` and `WINDOWS_SIGNING_PASSWORD` secrets exist. Until then the release notes say it's unsigned.
- **PRs:** the On-prem workflow checks the script parses, compiles the installer, and uploads the `.exe` so you can download it from the PR and try it.

**Testing.** I ran the script the way the installer lays it out, against Docker here:
- **Lifecycle:** install, then real data via the on-prem tests, backup, re-install over the top (secrets kept, backup made), restore (users, admin and accounts all came back), stop/start, uninstall keeping the data, reinstall picking it up, and uninstall deleting it.
- **Guards:** a taken port and a missing settings file both stop with their messages.
- **On-prem tests:** all 3 pass on the final stack.
- **Repo checks:** lint, formatting, the env, secrets and standards checks all pass, and the secrets scan of the new files found nothing.

**Two bugs found and fixed while testing:**
- **PowerShell 5.1:** it treats any stderr from `docker` as a fatal error, which made install fail when no data volume existed yet.
- **Name clash with your local Supabase:** both used the Compose project name `pairsight`, and the uninstaller's cleanup flag (`--remove-orphans`) could have removed Supabase containers. The stack is now `pairsight-onprem`, with its own volume names, and that flag is gone. Your Supabase containers were untouched throughout.

**Still to do:**
- **Hosted e2e and `check:rls`:** these need local Supabase, which is stopped as you asked. I'll run them with `/local-review` and `/e2e-review` before the PR.
- **Before the first real release:** make the GHCR image public once, and add the signing certificate when it's bought.

I've cleaned up my test stack, volumes and test secrets. Tell me when to commit PRs 3 and 4 and open the PR.

That's already what the branch does: PR 4 is an `.exe` installer (an `.msi` isn't needed), and I've now built one here. The customer installs Docker Desktop and WSL first, and setup does the rest.

**The build.** The installer used to be built only in CI, so I installed the tool that builds it (Inno Setup, just for your user account, no admin needed) and compiled it. The result is `deploy\windows\Output\PairSight-Setup-0.0.0-local.exe`, about 2 MB. It points at the PairSight image already on this machine, so it won't download anything.

**What I added for WSL.** If Docker Desktop won't start, setup now checks whether WSL is set up. If it isn't, setup says to run `wsl --install` as administrator, restart, then run setup again. Before this, a customer missing WSL only got "Docker Desktop didn't start". I've added the same to the install docs. On this machine the pre-install check passes, and the script still parses cleanly.

**What I haven't tested:** the `.exe` itself has not been run yet. It needs the Windows admin prompt, which only you can approve, so it's best tried by you as a customer would:

1. Double-click `deploy\windows\Output\PairSight-Setup-0.0.0-local.exe` and say Yes to the admin prompt. The publisher shows as unknown because the installer isn't signed yet.
2. A window shows it starting PairSight. The first start takes a minute or two.
3. At the end, tick "Open PairSight". The first account you create becomes the admin.
4. Try the Start menu items under PairSight: Open, Stop and Back up.
5. Uninstall it from Windows Settings, then Apps. It asks whether to delete the data, and the default keeps it.

If anything fails, the window says why. Tell me what it says, or I can read the logs.

**What a real customer needs that we don't have yet:**
- **Published image:** our installer downloads PairSight from GitHub's package registry, so the first release tag has to publish the image there and it has to be made public once.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=96 session=e83fc832-7425-43cd-8ced-50de50186402]

The on-prem image no longer ships our source or any source maps. A check at the end of the image build now fails it if any get back in. I rebuilt the image, and the on-prem tests pass on it (3 of 3), including a full run with Splink. Nothing is committed yet.

**What the audit found in the image:**
- **Source maps:** 37 for the app and about 1,100 inside its packages, plus comments in about 2,000 files pointing at them.
- **Splink's Python:** the plain `.py` files, plus its tests and dev files.
- **Start-up scripts:** both were readable, with comments, and the demo seed even had the source file paths in it.
- **Benchmark output:** 75 MB of Splink benchmark CSVs. These are ignored by git, so a clean CI build wouldn't include them, but any build on this machine did.

The web app itself was already minified.

**What I changed:**
- **Source maps:** a new step, `deploy/onprem/strip-source-maps.mjs`, deletes every map and the comments pointing at them, in the app and in the packages. It also drops the `services/` and `scripts/` folders that the build copies in whole.
- **Start-up scripts:** each is now one minified file with no comments or maps.
- **Python:** Splink is compiled to bytecode with docstrings dropped, and the `.py` files are deleted. It runs exactly as before.
- **Build check:** `deploy/onprem/check-no-source.sh` fails the build on any map, map comment, or our own TypeScript or Python. It skips other packages' own source, which they publish anyway. It caught real leftovers on its first runs before I fixed them.
- **No leftovers in image layers:** all of this happens before the files reach the image's final stage, so nothing removed survives in an earlier layer either.
- **Size:** the image went from 1.63 GB to 1.48 GB.
- **Docs:** there's a new "What the image ships" section in `docs/PairSight-OnPrem.md`.

**What stays readable, because it has to:**
- **Data model:** the database schema and migrations, which each start needs.
- **On-screen text** files.
- **Open-source packages.**

Minified code and bytecode put off casual reading, not a determined effort. Jev's judging, the most valuable part, never ships.

**Stronger options, if you want them:**

| Option | Protection | Cost | My take |
|---|---|---|---|
| Keep the source maps privately in CI | none, but lets us read customers' error traces | low | **Do it.** Without maps, a customer's stack trace is unreadable to us too. |
| Licence agreement shown by the installer | legal, the real protection | low | **Do it**, once you have the text. |
| Compile Splink's Python to native code (Cython) | strong: native code is much harder to read than bytecode | medium: one build step | **Worth it** if Splink's matching setup counts as our core logic. |
| Obfuscate the JavaScript (javascript-obfuscator) | moderate | high | **Skip it.** It slows the server, can break the app's internals, and makes customer bugs impossible to debug. |

My recommendation is the private maps and the licence now, and Cython if you count Splink's matching setup as core logic. Say which you'd like.

Your installed PairSight uses the `:dev` image, which I rebuilt, so its next start runs the new build. Its data wasn't touched.

The installer no longer ships the landing page or the demo. Local dev and the hosted site still have both, unchanged. Nothing is committed yet.

**How it works:**
- **The pages:** the landing page and the demo's pages now sit together in one route-group folder, `src/app/[locale]/(marketing)/`. Their URLs are unchanged. The installer's build deletes that folder before building, so those pages aren't in it at all.
- **Shared screens:** two app screens borrowed from the demo. The sign-in page showed the demo's results and a "Look at the demo first" link, and the empty dashboard showed a results preview and a "Try the demo" button. Both now get the demo through one small file, `demo-preview.ts`, and the installer's build switches that off, so the demo's recorded data is left out of the bundle. Without the demo, those screens show just their form or steps.
- **Start-up:** the installed app no longer loads the demo data into the customer's database. Visiting `/` takes you to Evaluations, which sends anyone signed out to sign in, and `/sandbox` shows "not found".
- **Copy:** the landing page's text is removed from what the image ships.

**Checks that keep it this way:**
- **Build check:** the image build now fails if the landing page, the demo's pages, its data or its text get back in. I ran the same search on a normal hosted build, and it finds them there, so the check does detect them.
- **New on-prem test:** `/` lands on sign-in with no demo link, `/sandbox` is a 404, and the empty dashboard has no "Try the demo" button. All 4 on-prem tests pass on the new image.
- **Screenshots:** I looked at the installed version's sign-in page and empty dashboard, and both look right without the demo.
- **Hosted build:** it still has the landing page and every demo page.
- **Repo checks:** types, lint, unit tests (386 pass), and the env, secrets, standards and formatting checks all pass.
- **Docs:** I added the rule to `AGENTS.md` and `docs/PairSight-OnPrem.md`. New marketing pages go in the `(marketing)` folder, and a shared screen that uses the demo does it behind the build switch.

**Things to know:**
- **Your installed PairSight** uses the image I rebuilt. When it next starts, it won't have the demo. The demo data it loaded the first time stays in its database, but no page shows it, so you can ignore it.
- **The `--if-missing` option** I'd added to the demo-loading script for on-prem is reverted, since nothing uses it now.

Setup and uninstall now match PairSight, following Windows' light or dark setting. I clicked through a preview build in both themes, including a full uninstall. The real installer, which needs the admin prompt, hasn't been run yet.

You already had an uninstaller: it's in Windows Settings → Apps and in the Start menu as "Uninstall PairSight". It's now themed and works better (below).

**Setup:**
- **Look:** the app's background colours (off-white or near-black), the bevel lines hidden, and our own artwork. That's a mosaic of the logo's vermilion, ink and ice tiles above the wordmark in the app's font, plus the logo top right on every other page. A script renders the art from the app's colours and fonts at each Windows scaling size, in both themes (`deploy/windows/art/render.mjs`).
- **Pages:** Welcome, then a desktop-shortcut choice, then "Checking this machine" with live progress. Ready lists what passed: ✓ Docker Desktop is running, ✓ 15 GB of memory, and so on. Then Installing, and Finished with "Open PairSight now".
- **No console window:** the setup steps used to run in a visible PowerShell window. They now run hidden and report each step ("Downloading PairSight…", "Starting PairSight…") inside the installer.
- **Errors in plain words:**
  - If the machine isn't ready, a dialog says why.
  - If PairSight doesn't start, the last page says why instead of "PairSight is ready", and the "Open PairSight now" box doesn't appear.
  - Download and start-up failures now say what to do, not which Docker command failed.
- **Copy:** every stock label I saw is replaced, from the window title to the "Stop setting up?" prompt.

**Uninstall:**
- **The data question:** two clear choices, "Keep my data" (the default, ready for a reinstall) or "Delete my data" (can't be undone). Backups are always kept. Progress shows in the uninstall window, in the same theme.
- **Fixed a real gap:** PairSight's containers restart with Docker. If Docker wasn't running, uninstalling quietly skipped stopping them, so PairSight could come back on the next start of Docker with nothing left to remove it. Uninstall now stops before removing anything and says to start Docker Desktop first. The same applies when uninstalling by hand.

**What I checked:**
- **Preview walkthroughs:** both themes went through every setup and uninstall page and cleaned up completely. The preview installs for your user only, with its install and uninstall steps swapped for a stand-in, so it never touched your PairSight. The window stayed responsive throughout.
- **Real script, hidden like the installer runs it:** the machine check, the status command, and Docker writing errors all behave.
- **Repo checks:** the installer compiles, the script parses, and the secrets, standards, lint and formatting checks pass.

**Still to do or know:**
- **The real installer needs your run.** It's at `deploy\windows\Output\PairSight-Setup-0.0.0-local.exe`. Running it updates your existing install: it backs up first and keeps your data and secrets.
- **CI version:** the theme needs Inno Setup 6.7. Both workflows now install 6.7.1, the newest Chocolatey has, but I've only compiled with 6.7.3 here, so the PR's CI run will be the real check.
- **Disk space:** this machine has 11 GB free on C:, just over setup's 10 GB minimum, partly because of my image builds.
- **Start menu shortcuts:** Open, Stop and Back up still open a PowerShell window while they run. I can theme or hide those too if you want.

**An apology:** my first preview run clicked through using simulated keypresses. You were using the machine, so a few Enter presses went to your Chrome window. I switched to a method that never touches focus or the keyboard and won't use keypresses again.

Nothing is committed yet.

Here's everything since the last devlog, which I wrote on 12 Oct before committing the redesign. Since then PRs #22 and #23 have merged. The rest of the on-prem work (the Docker image, the Compose stack and the Windows installer) is on `feat/onprem-compose` and isn't committed yet.

## At a glance

| | Before (12 Oct) | Now (14 Oct) |
|---|---|---|
| Ways to run PairSight | hosted only | **hosted, or installed on the customer's own Windows machine** |
| Who sets the Jev key | the server's `.env` | **an admin, in Settings** (encrypted, checked before it's saved, never shown again) |
| Services contacted from an on-prem install | n/a | **Jev only** (logged at start-up) |
| Installer | none | **`PairSight-Setup.exe`** with its own theme (light and dark), plus an uninstaller |
| On-prem image size | n/a | **1.48 GB** (2.74 GB on the first working version) |
| Source maps / readable source in the image | ~1,140 maps, plain Python | **0**, and the build fails if any get back in |
| Landing page and demo in the installer | n/a | **left out** (the hosted site and local dev still have them) |
| Tests | 376 unit, 37 full-flow + 31 public | **386 unit, 87 hosted e2e, 4 on-prem e2e** |
| Tables under the database-access check | 17 | **18** |

## 12 Oct: demo card and test cleanup
- **The demo shows "What we noticed in this file":**
  - **How:** the demo seed now profiles its files the same way a real upload is profiled.
  - **What it says:** the organisations and people demos say they're from the United Kingdom, going by their postcodes. The products demo says nothing needs special handling.
- **Throwaway accounts deleted:** I removed the 18 `e2e+ux…` audit accounts and their 16 draft evaluations in one transaction. Your regular e2e accounts weren't touched.

## 13 Oct: PR #22, the redesign (merged)
- **What:** 14 feature commits:
  - tokens and theme;
  - components;
  - top bar and dock;
  - country guess;
  - sample files;
  - each screen;
  - landing page;
  - copy;
  - tests.

  New tests cover Privacy, Terms and the sample downloads.
- **Two CI failures fixed:**
  - **Sign-in without a database:** the mosaic beside the sign-in form queried the database, so sign-in broke when the database was down. It now reads the demo's recorded run from a file.
  - **CI e2e runs a production build:** it used to test a dev server, which compiles each page on first visit and made the tests flaky.
- **Numbers:** 76 Chromium e2e tests, 6 motion tests in WebKit and Firefox, and all 8 CI checks green.
- **Owned:** one file deletion ended up in the session-log commit. I didn't force-push to rewrite history on the open PR.

## 13 Oct: on-prem plan, then PR #23 (merged)
- **The plan:** four PRs, using the answers you gave:
  - **Packaging:** Docker Desktop.
  - **Accounts:** local accounts in the customer's own database.
  - **Access:** this machine only.
  - **Jev key:** set in the app.
- **PR 1, the Jev key in Settings:**
  - **Storage:** the key is encrypted with AES-256-GCM in the new `jev_connections` table.
  - **Checking:** "Check and save" sends Jev one made-up pair first, and that check is costed like any other call.
  - **No key:** Check and start shows "Add a Jev key in Settings". A run stopped by a key problem links there too.
- **PR 2, the on-prem profile (`PAIRSIGHT_DEPLOYMENT=onprem`):**
  - **First admin:** the first account becomes the admin. Two people signing up at once can't both become admin.
  - **People in Settings:** admins can make someone an admin or remove them. The last admin can't be removed.
  - **Prices:** a bundled price list replaces the daily OpenRouter fetch.
  - **Start-up:** the server logs the outside services it will contact.
  - **Privacy page:** it has an on-prem version.
- **Your call:** on-prem ignores the server's `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`. You'd noticed runs working with no key saved, so now only the admin's saved key counts.
- **Bugs found:**
  - **Old key kept:** a run could keep using the old key for 30 seconds after a new one was saved. The remembered key is now shared between the worker and Settings.
  - **Flaky dashboard test:** it matched the toast's screen-reader text and is now an exact match.
- **Numbers:** 383 unit tests, 87 hosted e2e tests, the on-prem spec, and a new test proving every model has a price on-prem.
- **Owned:** one of my test runs went to your :3200 server and created one test account there. I deleted it.

## 13 Oct: on-prem PRs 3 and 4 (uncommitted)
- **Image (`deploy/onprem/Dockerfile`):**
  - **What's in it:** the web server, the run worker and Splink's Python. It runs as a non-root user.
  - **On each start:** it applies migrations and the one-off SQL, then serves.
- **Stack (`compose.yaml`):** Postgres, the sign-in server, Caddy and the app.
  - Only `127.0.0.1:3080` is published.
  - The browser never reaches the sign-in server directly.
- **Secrets:** each install's random secrets are made once. Setup refuses to make new ones over existing data, because that would lock the data out.
- **Windows script (`pairsight.ps1`):**
  - **Before installing**, it checks Docker Desktop, WSL, memory, disk and the port.
  - **Commands:** install, start, stop, status, logs, backup, restore, update and uninstall.
- **Release pipeline:** an `onprem-v*` tag publishes the image and a `.exe` pinned to it. The `.exe` is signed once the certificate secrets exist.
- **Fixes found while testing:**
  - **Database role:** the sign-in server needs a role named `postgres`.
  - **Run worker:** Next's standalone build drops the file that starts it, so runs hung until a build step copied it back.
  - **Prisma:** it needs OpenSSL at build time.
  - **PowerShell 5.1:** it treated Docker's stderr output as a fatal error.
  - **Name clash:** the stack shared the project name `pairsight` with your local Supabase, so uninstalling could have removed its containers. The stack is now `pairsight-onprem`.
- **Numbers:** the full lifecycle was tested against Docker here: install, back up, reinstall, restore, uninstall keeping the data, and uninstall deleting it. The test run of the sample files produced 210 pairs, 178 of them suggested by Splink inside the image.

## 14 Oct: installer polish (uncommitted)
- **Local `.exe`:** I installed Inno Setup for your user account, so the installer now builds here. It also tells the customer to run `wsl --install` when WSL is missing.
- **No source in the image:**
  - **Removed:** every source map and the comments pointing at them.
  - **Start-up scripts:** minified.
  - **Splink:** compiled to bytecode with docstrings dropped.
  - **Image:** 75 MB of benchmark CSVs gone, and the size went from 1.63 GB to 1.48 GB.
  - **Guard:** `check-image.sh` fails the build if any of it comes back.
  - **Your call:** no further hardening for now.
- **No marketing in the installer:**
  - **Pages:** the landing page and demo moved to `src/app/[locale]/(marketing)/`, which the on-prem build deletes. Their URLs are unchanged.
  - **Shared screens:** sign-in and the empty dashboard drop their demo parts behind one build switch.
  - **Routes:** `/` goes to sign-in, and `/sandbox` shows "not found".
  - **Rule:** the new rule is written down in AGENTS.md.
- **Themed installer and uninstaller:**
  - **Look:** the app's colours in light and dark, with mosaic and wordmark art rendered from the app's tokens.
  - **Progress:** a "Checking this machine" page, and a Ready page listing what passed.
  - **Errors:** setup steps run hidden and failures read in plain words.
  - **Uninstall:** a "Keep my data / Delete my data" choice. It now refuses to run while Docker is stopped; before, PairSight could come back the next time Docker started.
- **Numbers:** 4 of 4 on-prem e2e tests pass. Preview builds were walked through every page in both themes.
- **Owned:** my first preview walkthrough sent Enter keypresses that reached your Chrome window. I've stopped using keypresses.

## Decisions you made
- **Packaging:** Docker Desktop, local accounts, this machine only, and the Jev key set in the app.
- **Server key:** on-prem ignores it.
- **One branch:** PRs 3 and 4 share `feat/onprem-compose`.
- **No extra hardening for now:** no private source maps in CI, no licence screen in the installer, no Cython and no JS obfuscation.
- **No marketing in the installer:** the landing page and demo stay hosted and in local dev only.
- **Installer:** themed Inno Setup rather than a custom installer app.

## Still open
- **Your test:** run `deploy\windows\Output\PairSight-Setup-0.0.0-local.exe`, which needs the admin prompt. It updates your existing install and keeps your data.
- **CI check:** CI installs Inno Setup 6.7.1, but I've only compiled with 6.7.3 here, so the PR's run is the real check.
- **Before the first release:**
  - make the GHCR image public;
  - buy the signing certificate;
  - decide how passwords get reset with no email server.
- **Offered:** hiding the console window that the Start menu shortcuts open.
- **Earlier items:** the company's legal name, a legal review of Privacy and Terms, Security and Contact pages, and the "All caught up" card.
- **This machine:** 11 GB free on C:.
- **To commit:** PRs 3 and 4 on `feat/onprem-compose`.
